=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer sequence A=(A_1,A_2,\ldots,A_N) of length N.
For each integer pair (L,R) with 1 \le L \le R \le N, define f(L,R) as follows:

- Start with an empty blackboard. Write the R-L+1 integers A_L, A_{L+1}, \ldots, A_R on the blackboard in order.
- Repeat the following operation until all integers on the blackboard are erased:
- Choose integers l, r with l \le r such that every integer from l through r appears at least once on the blackboard. Then, erase all integers from l through r that are on the blackboard.


- Let f(L,R) be the minimum number of such operations needed to erase all the integers from the blackboard.

Find \displaystyle \sum_{L=1}^N \sum_{R=L}^N f(L,R).

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \le N \le 3 \times 10^5
- 1 \le A_i \le N
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4
1 3 1 4

Sample Output 1

16

For example, in the case of (L,R)=(1,4):

- The blackboard has 1,3,1,4.
- Choose (l,r)=(1,1) and erase all occurrences of 1. The blackboard now has 3,4.
- Choose (l,r)=(3,4) and erase all occurrences of 3 and 4. The blackboard becomes empty.
- It cannot be done in fewer than two operations, so f(1,4) = 2.

Similarly, you can find f(2,4)=2, f(1,1)=1, etc.
\displaystyle \sum_{L=1}^N \sum_{R=L}^N f(L,R) = 16, so print 16.

Sample Input 2

5
3 1 4 2 4

Sample Output 2

23

Sample Input 3

10
5 1 10 9 2 5 6 9 1 6

Sample Output 3

129

- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Option 1: positions lists + bisect. Python bisect on list of ints is C? bisect module implemented in Python? Actually bisect is C? In CPython, _bisect C module used, so fast. O(N log N) ~ 5 million operations, fine. Need also build positions lists. N=3e5, memory okay.

But we can compute q_left with last_pos[x-1] while scanning; q_right with positions[x-1] and bisect_right. Need prev_x with last_pos[x]. This is simple.

Pseudo:

import sys, bisect
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    pos = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]
    for idx, val in enumerate(A, start=1):
        pos[val].append(idx)
    last = [0]*(N+1)
    ans = 0
    for i, x in enumerate(A, start=1):
        prev_x = last[x]
        q_left = last[x-1] if x > 1 else 0  # last[x-1] for x=1 is last[0]=0 anyway if array size N+1; value 0 not used. Could just last[x-1] since last[0]=0.
        lst = pos[x-1] if x > 1 else []? If x=1, x-1=0, pos[0] empty. We can allocate N+1 and pos[0]=[].
        idx = bisect.bisect_right(lst, i)
        q_right = lst[idx] if idx < len(lst) else N+1
        left_choices = i - max(prev_x, q_left)
        right_choices = q_right - i
        ans += left_choices * right_choices
        last[x] = i
    print(ans)

Need ensure q_left = last[x-1] is nearest occurrence of x-1 before i. Since last array updated as we scan. At i, last[x-1] is latest occurrence of x-1 among positions < i (because if current x != x-1, no update yet for x-1 at i; if current x = x-1? But x = A_i, x-1 different, so not. Good). If x=1, last[0] remains 0. Good.

q_right = first occurrence of x-1 after i. positions[x-1] sorted; bisect_right returns index after i. Since i not in positions[x-1] (because A_i=x, not x-1), bisect_right = bisect_left. If x=1, pos[0] empty, q_right=N+1.

prev_x = last[x] before updating. Good.

Let's test edge cases.

N=1, A=[1].

Implementation with pointer method:

import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    pos = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    for idx, val in enumerate(A, 1):
        pos[val].append(idx)

    last = [0] * (N + 1)
    ptr = [0] * (N + 1)  # ptr[v] = index of first position of v >= current i
    ans = 0

    for i, x in enumerate(A, 1):
        # previous occurrence of x
        prev_x = last[x]
        # nearest occurrence of x-1 before i
        left_forbidden = last[x - 1]  # last[0]=0
        # nearest occurrence of x-1 after i
        lst = pos[x - 1]
        p = ptr[x - 1]
        right_forbidden = lst[p] if p < len(lst) else N + 1

        left_choices = i - (prev_x if prev_x > left_forbidden else left_forbidden)
        right_choices = right_forbidden - i
        ans += left_choices * right_choices

        last[x] = i
        ptr[x] += 1

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Use `data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to parse fast. Then N=data[0], A=data[1:]. This is common. Let's use buffer read for speed.

Code:

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    pos = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    for i, v in enumerate(A, 1):
        pos[v].append(i)

    last = [0] * (N + 1)
    ptr = [0] * (N + 1)
    ans = 0

    for i, x in enumerate(A, 1):
        prev_x = last[x]
        left_forbidden = last[x - 1]

        lst = pos[x - 1]
        p = ptr[x - 1]
        right_forbidden = lst[p] if p < len(lst) else N + 1

        left_choices = i - (prev_x if prev_x > left_forbidden else left_forbidden)
        right_choices = right_forbidden - i
        ans += left_choices * right_choices

        last[x] = i
        ptr[x] += 1

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let's maybe derive the pointer method more rigorously to ensure no off-by-one with `ptr` at current i:
We define `ptr[v]` as the index in `pos[v]` of the first occurrence whose position is at least `i` at the beginning of iteration `i`. Initially i=1, first occurrence at least 1 is index 0 for all v (if any). At iteration i:
- For v = x-1, since position i is not in pos[v], first occurrence at least i is actually first occurrence > i. So `pos[v][ptr[v]]` is the next occurrence after i. If ptr[v] == len, no such.
- After processing, for v != x, if pos[v] contains i, then v=x, contradiction. So first occurrence at least i+1 is same as at least i. For v=x, ptr[x] currently points to i (because i is in pos[x] and it's the first occurrence at least i). Incrementing makes it first occurrence at least i+1. Thus invariant holds.
This is correct.

Now, let's think about if `ptr[x]` currently points to i if there is an earlier occurrence of x? Wait if there is an earlier occurrence, lower_bound(pos[x], i) still returns i because i is in list and is after earlier occurrences. Example pos[x]=[2,5], i=5, ptr[x] after previous increments: after i=2 ptr=1 (pos5). At i=5, ptr=1 points to5. Good. If i=2, ptr=0 points to2. Good. If i is an occurrence, lower_bound returns that occurrence. So yes.

Now, let's consider if `last[x]` and `ptr[x]` both updated; last stores latest position, ptr stores next index. They are independent.

Current A_i=x, so i not in pos[x-1]. Thus lower_bound(pos[x-1], i) cannot return i. It returns first position > i. Good. So no need to check equality. If due to invariant bug, but no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "memory" if `data` list and `pos` lists both store ints; okay. Could delete data after building pos? We need data for second loop. We could build pos and then iterate over data; no need to delete. If memory concern, we could store A separately and delete data? Not necessary. But maybe we can avoid data list by reading A list and then using it; same memory. Let's estimate with data + pos + last + ptr:
- data: list of N+1 references to int objects. The int objects for values from map are created; data holds them. Values are 1..N, but each int object distinct. 300001*28 ≈ 8.4MB + list refs 2.4MB = 10.8MB.
- pos: outer list refs 300001*8=2.4MB; 300001 empty list objects? Wait `[[] for _ in range(N+1)]` creates N+1 distinct empty list objects, each 56 bytes = 16.8MB. Nonempty lists have additional allocated arrays for appended indices. Each appended index is an int object created by `range`? In Python, `range` yields a new PyLong each iteration? Actually for i in range, i is a Python int object; when appended to pos, it stores reference to that object. The loop variable i is rebound each iteration; the previous int object remains referenced in pos. So 300k PyLong objects for indices = 8.4MB. List slots for indices: each list has capacity; total slots maybe >N, each slot 8 bytes, ~2.4MB plus overallocation. Empty list objects 16.8MB. Total pos maybe 30MB.
- last: list of N+1 refs to int 0 initially (same object). When updated, stores references to i int objects from second loop? Wait in second loop, `for i in range(1, N+1)` creates new int objects for i. We assign `last[x] = i`, so last holds references to those i objects. But note the same i object is also used? In second loop, i is a Python int object; when we assign to last, it keeps it alive. At next iteration, i variable rebinds to new int; previous remains in last. So last may hold up to N distinct int objects for positions (8.4MB) plus list refs 2.4MB. However, if a value appears multiple times, last only holds latest position, so at most number of distinct values positions, ≤N. So ~10.8MB.
- ptr: list of N+1 refs initially 0. When `ptr[x] += 1`, creates new int objects for counts. At most N updates, but ptr holds one count per value, sum of counts? Actually ptr[v] is index of next occurrence; for values with occurrences, it holds an int between 0 and count. At end, many values have count len(pos[v]) maybe >256, distinct int objects. Up to N distinct values, ~8.4MB + list refs 2.4MB =10.8MB.
Total ~60MB. Fine.

Can we reduce memory significantly? Use `pos` as list of lists is main. Could use dictionary mapping value to list only for values that appear. But we need pos[x-1] for x-1 possibly absent; dict get returns empty list. Number of distinct values ≤N, but if many values absent, dict saves empty list objects. Could use arrays of head/next linked lists to avoid pos lists? We can compute q_right with next occurrence arrays? Let's explore if memory limit unknown.

We can compute q_right for each position using a single pass and an array `next_pos`? Actually we need for each i, next occurrence of A_i-1. We can precompute an array `next_less[i]` by scanning from right to left maintaining last occurrence of each value? Let's see:
We want for each position i, `next_less[i] = nearest position > i with value A_i-1`. We can compute in a right-to-left pass:
Initialize `last_seen[v] = N+1` for all v.
For i from N down to 1:
    x = A[i]
    next_less[i] = last_seen[x-1]
    last_seen[x] = i
This gives for each position i the nearest occurrence of x-1 to the right. Great! We don't need pos lists or ptr. Then we can compute answer in left-to-right pass using `last` for prev_x and q_left, and `next_less[i]` for q_right. This uses O(N) memory and simpler? Let's examine.

We need q_right for each i: nearest position > i with value A_i-1. We can compute by scanning A from right to left:
last_pos = [N+1]*(N+1)
next_less = [N+1]*(N+1) maybe length N+1 for 1-index positions.
For i from N down to 1:
    x = A[i] (if using 0-index or 1-index)
    next_less[i] = last_pos[x-1]
    last_pos[x] = i
This is correct because last_pos[x-1] at that time is nearest occurrence of x-1 to the right (since we've processed positions > i). For x=1, last_pos[0] remains N+1. Then left-to-right:
last = [0]*(N+1)
ans=0
for i in 1..N:
    x=A[i]
    prev_x = last[x]
    q_left = last[x-1]
    q_right = next_less[i]
    ans += (i - max(prev_x, q_left)) * (q_right - i)
    last[x] = i
This avoids pos lists and ptr entirely! Much simpler and memory efficient. Let's verify with samples.

Sample1 A=[1,3,1,4].
Right-to-left:
last_pos initially [5,5,5,5,5]? N+1=5.
i=4 x=4: next_less[4]=last_pos[3]=5; last_pos[4]=4.
i=3 x=1: next_less[3]=last_pos[0]=5; last_pos[1]=3.
i=2 x=3: next_less[2]=last_pos[2]=5; last_pos[3]=2.
i=1 x=1: next_less[1]=last_pos[0]=5; last_pos[1]=1.
Left-to-right:
i=1 x=1 prev0 q_left last0=0 q_right5 =>4.
i=2 x=3 prev0 q_left last2=0 q_right5 =>6.
i=3 x=1 prev1 q_left0 q_right5 =>4.
i=4 x=4 prev0 q_left last3=2 q_right5 =>2. total16. Good.

This is even better! Let's adopt this approach. It eliminates pos lists and ptr, uses two passes and arrays `next_less` of length N+1 (or N+2). Need store A. We can use 1-indexed A list. Memory: A list N+1 ints, next_less N+1 ints, last N+1 ints. Much less. Time O(N). Code simpler.

Let's implement with `data`:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = [0] + data[1:]  # 1-indexed
    # data can be freed? If we do `del data` after A, A holds references to int objects from data slice? Actually A = [0] + data[1:] creates a new list with references to the same int objects in data[1:]. If we delete data, the int objects remain referenced by A. Good.
    del data
    next_less = [N + 1] * (N + 1)  # indices 0..N, next_less[0] unused
    last_pos = [N + 1] * (N + 1)   # values 0..N
    for i in range(N, 0, -1):
        x = A[i]
        next_less[i] = last_pos[x - 1]
        last_pos[x] = i

    last = [0] * (N + 1)
    ans = 0
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        x = A[i]
        prev_x = last[x]
        left_forbidden = last[x - 1]
        right_forbidden = next_less[i]
        left_choices = i - (prev_x if prev_x > left_forbidden else left_forbidden)
        right_choices = right_forbidden - i
        ans += left_choices * right_choices
        last[x] = i
    print(ans)

At iteration i, before updating `last_pos[x]`, `last_pos[x-1]` is nearest occurrence of x-1 to the right. Then we set `last_pos[x]=i` for future left positions. Good. For x=1, `last_pos[0]` remains N+1 because never updated. Good.

Now, let's maybe write final code with comments:

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data
    A[0] = 0  # 1-indexed values

    # next_less[i] = nearest position > i whose value is A[i]-1
    next_less = [N + 1] * (N + 1)
    last_pos = [N + 1] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(N, 0, -1):
        x = A[i]
        next_less[i] = last_pos[x - 1]
        last_pos[x] = i

    last = [0] * (N + 1)
    ans = 0
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        x = A[i]
        prev_x = last[x]
        left_forbidden = last[x - 1]
        right_forbidden = next_less[i]

        left_bound = prev_x if prev_x > left_forbidden else left_forbidden
        ans += (i - left_bound) * (right_forbidden - i)

        last[x] = i

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

i=2 x=3 next_less[2]=last_pos[2]=5; last_pos[3]=2
i=1 x=1 next_less[1]=last_pos[0]=5; last_pos[1]=1
Left pass:

In CPython, range iterator returns a new PyLong for values > small cache. So there are N int objects for positions, referenced by last_pos/next_less. But last_pos only stores latest occurrence per value, however next_less stores one position per index, so up to N references. The int objects for positions that are not latest for their value may not be referenced except maybe in next_less? Actually every position i is stored in last_pos[x] at time of processing, but later if same x occurs to the left, last_pos[x] overwritten with new i, so the old position int object may still be referenced by next_less entries for positions to the left? Let's analyze: Position p (1-indexed) is a Python int object created when range reaches p. At iteration p, `last_pos[A[p]] = p`. This object may later be copied into `next_less[j]` for some j < p where A[j] = A[p]+1. If A[p] is overwritten in last_pos by a later occurrence to the left? Actually later in right pass means smaller i. If there is another occurrence of same value at q < p, then at iteration q, last_pos[A[q]] = q, overwriting p. But p may still be referenced by next_less entries set before q? For j between q+1 and p-1, if A[j] = A[p]+1, next_less[j] was set to p before q? Wait right pass goes from N down. At iteration p, set last_pos[A[p]]=p. For j in (q, p) (i.e., positions between q and p), when processed later (since j > q? Actually right pass descending: after p, process p-1, ..., q+1 before q. During those iterations, last_pos[A[p]] is p, so next_less[j] may reference p. Then at q, last_pos overwritten to q. So p object remains referenced by next_less for those j. Thus many position int objects remain. Memory still O(N). Fine.

Could avoid storing data list by using array module, but not needed.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "last_pos = [N + 1] * (N + 1)" and then `next_less[i] = last_pos[x - 1]`; if last_pos[x-1] is N+1, next_less stores reference to same N+1 int object. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "last = [0] * (N + 1)" and `last[x] = i` stores i int objects from left pass range. The left pass range creates new int objects for i; these are stored in last. The right pass range int objects are separate. So memory maybe additional N int objects for last. But okay. Could reuse data? no.

Because we scan from right to left, the first occurrence we see is the rightmost, then as we move left, we update to closer occurrences. At iteration i, after processing all positions >i, last_pos[x-1] is the closest occurrence to i among positions >i. Good.
